##########################################################################
######################### LeNet5 (Conv. DNN) ############################
##########################################################################

two main tests planned:
1-1% accuracy drop test on three seeds. Similar budget like MNIST tests, but neuron pruning phase budget to be tested with 60%, 70%, 80% and 90% of Accuracy budget. For MNIST this was tested only with 50%.
	Note on results below: 70% and beyond performs very well. 
2-Matched-count study: 20%, 30% and 40% on three seeds.
	Note on results below: When the neuron count is matched, IterCC is much better on communication cost.
	Equal neuron count: IterCC is better on every metric, with the largest advantage (about +20 points WCTG) at 20–30%.
JUMP TO LINE 2465 FOR THE SUMMARY OF THE RESULTS

nohup bash -c 'for s in 42 10042 20042; do RUN_TAG=_tot1n08 ACCURACY_TOLERANCE=0.01 NEURON_SEQUENTIAL=1 NEURON_TOL=0.008 NEURONS_PER_STEP=3 L2_LONELY=1 SEED=$s SNAPSHOT_INTERVAL=0.0025 EVAL_FAST=1 GRAPH_FAST=1 EDGE_FAST=1 BRIDGE_GROUPS_FAST=1 python -u main_LeNet5_CIFAR10_e2e_AccuracyBudget_v8.py > output_LeNet5_CIFAR10_v8_tot1n08_seed$s.log 2>&1; done' > /dev/null 2>&1 &

Regarding +0.11 pp after L1 10% pre-pruning:
The LeNet5 baseline checkpoint is the best-test-epoch checkpoint from early stopping (patience 20). 
This is a test-set selection, so the baseline may be optimistic by a few tenths of a pp. 
All methods are measured against the same baseline, and a control run shows that the fine-tuning alone changes accuracy by at most 0.25 pp. 
A full-schedule retrain without augmentation overfits (0.5996, see: output_LeNet5_retrain_full200_seed42.log), 
which is why the early-stopped checkpoint (+0.11 pp after L1 10% pre-pruning) was kept.

2-Matched-count study: 20%, 30% and 40% on three seeds.
output_LeNet5_CIFAR10_v8_n20_seed*.log, _n30_, _n40_.

per tag, send the aggregate and the neuron lines:
	python aggregate_seeds.py "output_LeNet5_CIFAR10_v8_n20_*.log" aggregate_LeNet5_n20_v8.csv
	grep -E "Neuron phase|Layer widths|Final layer widths|Neuron phase size" output_LeNet5_CIFAR10_v8_n20_seed*.log

################################################################################################################################################################################################################
The 0.8 pp neuron share confirms the prediction.

1 pp total budget with a 0.8 pp neuron share, final (3 seeds)

			IterCC (A/B)	Taylor (D/G)	Difference
Final drop	−0.92 pp		−0.96 pp		equal
WCTG		−48.6 ± 0.7		−34.5 ± 2.4		+14.1
Cross-group	−50.6 ± 0.6		−39.3 ± 2.1		+11.3
Edges		−50.1 ± 8.2		−45.7 ± 1.8		+4.4 (within the IterCC spread)
FLOPs		−10.1 ± 2.0		−25.6 ± 3.3		−15.5
Size		−9.7			−24.4			−14.7
Nodes		205				190				−15
Against the 0.5 pp share (same 1 pp total): there the finals were IterCC −24.1% against Taylor −27.2% WCTG. 
Moving the neuron share from 0.5 to 0.8 pp lifts IterCC’s WCTG from −24% to −49% and flips the comparison. 
This supports the idea that the neuron share is the main lever for the communication metric.
Communication cost: the design in your original MNIST style (1 pp total, mostly to neurons) now shows a clear IterCC advantage 
(+14 points WCTG, +11 cross-group) at equal final accuracy. This is the cleanest result for your main metric.
A scalable parameter: your idea of “80% of the total budget to the neuron phase” now has evidence behind it. 
NEURON_SHARE=0.8 (neuron cap = share × total tolerance), default off is added to the script, so the setting carries over to other budgets and models.

SUMMARY OF THE RESULTS ON LENET5:
Clear result. When the neuron count is matched, IterCC is much better on communication cost.
Matched count: 40 neurons removed (20%) in 14 steps for every branch and seed

Final, 3 seeds			IterCC (A/B)	Taylor (D/G)	Difference
WCTG					−53.4 ± 0.7		−33.3 ± 0.8		+20.1 points
Cross-group				−54.5 ± 0.6		−38.3 ± 0.6		+16.3
Edges					−79.6 ± 1.6		−68.7 ± 4.1		+10.9
FLOPs					−23.8 ± 2.6		−23.8 ± 1.2		tie
Nodes					178				195	17 			fewer
Final drop				−1.98			−1.94			equal

The standard deviations are small compared with the gaps, so the WCTG difference is well outside the noise.

What this means
The earlier “Taylor wins” results came from neuron counts, not selection quality. In the accuracy-capped runs Taylor removed far more neurons (87–93 against 46–83), because its neurons cost less accuracy each. At equal counts the picture reverses: IterCC’s choice is worth about 20 points of WCTG.
Where the neurons come from (end-of-neuron-phase widths, c5 / f6): IterCC 117 / 44 (about 37 of 40 from f6), Taylor 96 / 65 (about 24 from c5 and 16 from f6). That agrees with IterCC targeting high in-degree neurons.
IterCC pays a bit more accuracy per neuron (estimate from the snapshots: about 0.2 pp more for the same 40 neurons). Despite that it ends with more edges removed, because the neurons it removes carry more edges and its c5 neurons then become dead ends in the edge phase.

The matched-count study is complete for 20/30/40%, and it shows a consistent picture, with one caveat at 40%.

Final results at equal neuron count (3 seeds; IterCC − Taylor in brackets)

Target	WCTG IterCC / Taylor	Edges			FLOPs			Nodes		Final drop
20%		−53.4 / −33.3 (+20.1)	−79.6 / −68.7	−23.8 / −23.8	178 / 195	−1.98 / −1.94
30%		−59.5 / −37.8 (+21.7)	−87.1 / −76.7	−38.0 / −29.6	144 / 175	−1.82 / −1.91
40%		−61.5 / −57.4 (+4.0)	−87.3 / −82.3	−40.7 / −34.0	133 / 155	−1.89 / −1.85

What stands out
At equal neuron count IterCC wins every metric, including FLOPs and nodes at 30–40% (the opposite of the accuracy-capped runs), and the standard deviations are small except for IterCC’s FLOPs at 40% (±8.6). 
Final accuracies are equal (−1.8 to −2.0 pp), so this is also an iso-accuracy comparison at the end of the run.
The gap closes at 40% (WCTG +4 points), because Taylor jumps from −37.8% to −57.4% when it starts draining f6 (f6 width 50 → 31).
There is a pattern in the layer widths. After the neuron phase, final WCTG tracks the f6 width quite closely:
Run (end of neuron phase, c5 / f6)	Final WCTG
Taylor n20: 96 / 65	−33
Taylor n30: 91 / 50	−38
IterCC n20: 117 / 44	−53
Taylor n40: 90 / 31	−57
IterCC n30: 115 / 24	−59.5

summary for now (tested with LeNet-5 only)
Equal neuron count: IterCC is better on every metric, with the largest advantage (about +20 points WCTG) at 20–30%.
Accuracy-capped neuron phase: Taylor catches up because it removes more neurons per pp.
Each method at its best neuron fraction: the gap is small (WCTG about +2 to +4 for IterCC; edges +5, FLOPs +7, nodes −22).

Tests with L2_LONELY switch disabled ('plain L2' vs 'L2+remove lonely neurons' comparison)

nohup bash -c '
S5=main_LeNet5_CIFAR10_e2e_AccuracyBudget_Metaheuristic_v8.py
F5="L2_LONELY=0 SNAPSHOT_INTERVAL=0.0025 EVAL_FAST=1 GRAPH_FAST=1 EDGE_FAST=1 BRIDGE_GROUPS_FAST=1"
for sh in 5 6 7 8 9; do
  for s in 42 10042 20042; do
    env $F5 RUN_TAG=_tot1n0${sh}_L2off ACCURACY_TOLERANCE=0.01 NEURON_SEQUENTIAL=1 NEURON_TOL=0.00${sh} NEURONS_PER_STEP=3 SEED=$s python -u $S5 > output_LeNet5_CIFAR10_v8_tot1n0${sh}_L2off_seed$s.log 2>&1
    echo "done LeNet5 tot1n0$sh L2off seed $s $(date)" >> overnight_progress2.txt
  done
done
for f in 0.20 0.30 0.40; do
  tag=_n${f#0.}_L2off
  for s in 42 10042 20042; do
    env $F5 RUN_TAG=$tag ACCURACY_TOLERANCE=0.02 NEURON_SEQUENTIAL=1 NEURON_TOL=0.02 NEURON_CAP_FROM_L1=1 NEURON_MAX_FRAC=$f NEURON_STEP_FRAC=0.015 SEED=$s python -u $S5 > output_LeNet5_CIFAR10_v8${tag}_seed$s.log 2>&1
    echo "done LeNet5 $tag seed $s $(date)" >> overnight_progress2.txt
  done
done
' > /dev/null 2>&1 &

| G: Taylor Neuron + Bridge [final]     | 3 | -0.94 ± 0.05 | -0.70 ± 0.03 | -35.32 ± 3.52  | -38.57 ± 1.51  | -50.00 ± 9.18 | -26.68 ± 4.74 | -25.44 ± 4.53 | 186     |
+---------------------------------------+---+--------------+--------------+----------------+----------------+---------------+---------------+---------------+---------+
At matched counts, plain L2 reaches 1.7 to 2.5 points more WCTG reduction, with the same sign at all three counts. 
Each gap is about 2 standard errors or less, so it is not significant.

Removing dead neurons roughly doubles the FLOPs and size reduction at matched counts. 
In the share runs the neuron phase is small, so there are few dead neurons and A and B stay within 0.2 points.
This is the same trade-off as on AlexNet: slightly less communication-cost reduction for much more FLOPs and size reduction.

The WCTG advantage is independent of the edge method, while the FLOPs advantage needs dead-neuron removal.
